Dialogue 04: The outside images have not arrived
Original fictional setting: An assistant checks an external-record request with the practice's records coordinator.
Professional roles: Assistant / Records coordinator
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Assistant: The patient says their previous practice sent radiographs yesterday. I don't see them in the chart for today's consultation.
Records coordinator: I can check the transfer status. Do we have the verified patient record and the completed request?
Assistant: Yes, both are in our system. The request is recorded as sent, but I can't find a receipt confirmation.
Records coordinator: Then we know the request left us, not that the images arrived. I'll check the approved transfer channel.
Assistant: The patient thinks we must already have reviewed them. What can I accurately tell them while you check?
Records coordinator: Say the records team is confirming receipt. Don't imply that the dentist has seen or interpreted images we haven't located.
Assistant: Would their absence automatically mean another image will be taken here today, or is that a separate decision?
Records coordinator: That's a separate clinical decision for the dentist. A missing transfer does not establish a new imaging order.
Assistant: I'll pass on the patient's expectation and the current transfer status. Should I contact the previous practice again?
Records coordinator: Please let me handle that, so we avoid duplicate requests. I'll update the chart's transfer status after checking.
Assistant: Thanks. I'll tell the dentist receipt is unconfirmed and make no claim about the images' findings.
Records coordinator: Exactly. I'll report what actually arrives and whether any requested records are still outstanding.
Vocabulary in this exchange: consultation: A visit for discussing or assessing a concern with the appropriate dental professional. / imaging order: The authorized request specifying the imaging needed for a patient.
END REFERENCE

REFERENCE
Course: Dental Assisting English

Dialogue 05: A quieter discussion before deciding
Original fictional setting: A patient tells an assistant what would help them understand the proposed treatment discussion.
Professional roles: Patient / Assistant
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Patient: I couldn't follow everything the dentist said while the equipment was running in the next room. Could we go over it?
Assistant: Certainly. Which part would you like the dentist to explain again: the proposed treatment, the alternatives, or the timing?
Patient: The alternatives. I heard the word crown, but I don't understand why that option was mentioned for me.
Assistant: I can pass on that exact question. The dentist needs to explain the reason and the alternatives for your case.
Patient: Could my sister stay for that discussion? She helps me remember questions, but I want to answer for myself.
Assistant: I'll tell the dentist you want her support and time to give your own answers. We can check the arrangement together.
Patient: Thank you. I also need a slower explanation. Nodding earlier didn't mean I had understood all of it.
Assistant: I'll make that clear. Would it help if we list the questions aloud before the dentist returns?
Patient: Yes: why a crown was proposed, what the alternatives are, and what remains undecided after today's discussion.
Assistant: Those are the three questions I'll pass on. I won't describe a proposed option as a decision you've made.
Patient: That would help me feel less rushed. I don't need a promise; I need a clear explanation.
Assistant: I understand. I'll ask for the discussion to continue with those questions before anyone records a decision.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 06: The appointment date and the chart date differ
Original fictional setting: An assistant checks a documentation discrepancy with the records lead before a handoff.
Professional roles: Assistant / Records lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Assistant: The referral summary lists the consultation as May fourteenth, but our appointment record shows May seventeenth. Which date is verified?
Records lead: Before choosing either one, check whether they're describing the same event. One could be the referral date.
Assistant: That's possible. The summary column only says 'date,' so I can't tell what event the entry refers to.
Records lead: Then request clarification from its source. Don't change our clinical chart just to make the two documents look consistent.
Assistant: I'll ask whether May fourteenth is the referral date or the consultation date. The dentist needs the distinction for today's review.
Records lead: Good. Mark the query as pending and keep the verified appointment entry separate from the unclear summary.
Assistant: If the source confirms an error, should I replace the earlier text or use the amendment process?
Records lead: Use our amendment process. The correction needs a clear connection to the original entry and the verified information.
Assistant: I also sent the summary in yesterday's handoff. Updating the chart alone won't tell the dentist about this query.
Records lead: Exactly. Notify the dentist through the approved channel that the date's meaning is still being checked.
Assistant: I'll say the records differ, not that the patient gave us a wrong date.
Records lead: That's accurate. Report the evidence and the unresolved question without assigning responsibility before we know what happened.
Vocabulary in this exchange: consultation: A visit for discussing or assessing a concern with the appropriate dental professional. / clinical chart: The authorized record of a patient's dental information and care. / amendment: A documented correction or addition made through the approved record process.
END REFERENCE

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Dialogue 06: The appointment date and the chart date differ
Original fictional setting: An assistant checks a documentation discrepancy with the records lead before a handoff.
Professional roles: Assistant / Records lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Assistant: The referral summary lists the consultation as May fourteenth, but our appointment record shows May seventeenth. Which date is verified?
Records lead: Before choosing either one, check whether they're describing the same event. One could be the referral date.
Assistant: That's possible. The summary column only says 'date,' so I can't tell what event the entry refers to.
Records lead: Then request clarification from its source. Don't change our clinical chart just to make the two documents look consistent.
Assistant: I'll ask whether May fourteenth is the referral date or the consultation date. The dentist needs the distinction for today's review.
Records lead: Good. Mark the query as pending and keep the verified appointment entry separate from the unclear summary.
Assistant: If the source confirms an error, should I replace the earlier text or use the amendment process?
Records lead: Use our amendment process. The correction needs a clear connection to the original entry and the verified information.
Assistant: I also sent the summary in yesterday's handoff. Updating the chart alone won't tell the dentist about this query.
Records lead: Exactly. Notify the dentist through the approved channel that the date's meaning is still being checked.
Assistant: I'll say the records differ, not that the patient gave us a wrong date.
Records lead: That's accurate. Report the evidence and the unresolved question without assigning responsibility before we know what happened.
Vocabulary in this exchange: consultation: A visit for discussing or assessing a concern with the appropriate dental professional. / clinical chart: The authorized record of a patient's dental information and care. / amendment: A documented correction or addition made through the approved record process.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 07: A crown case needs a revised estimate
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 07: A crown case needs a revised estimate
Original fictional setting: An assistant calls a laboratory coordinator after the dentist submits a query on a returned crown case.
Professional roles: Assistant / Lab coordinator
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Assistant: Dr. Lee sent a query on crown case eighty-six this morning. Have you received the case reference and the clinician's comments?
Lab coordinator: Yes, the query arrived. Our review is pending; we haven't confirmed whether further work is needed yet.
Assistant: Reception is asking for a new appointment date. Can you give a revised turnaround estimate at this stage?
Lab coordinator: Not reliably until the review is complete. I can commit to a status update tomorrow at eleven.
Assistant: I'll distinguish that update from a delivery date. Does the query require any further information from the dentist?
Lab coordinator: The shade specification needs clarification. I'll send the exact question through the practice's approved channel, linked to this case.
Assistant: Please do. I can coordinate the response, but I won't choose a shade or alter the laboratory prescription myself.
Lab coordinator: Understood. Once the clinician clarifies it, we'll confirm how that affects the review and any proposed schedule.
Assistant: Should the returned case remain described as 'under review,' rather than 'remake agreed,' in our scheduling update?
Lab coordinator: Yes. A remake and any associated charge are not agreed simply because we've received a query.
Assistant: The lead assistant will be your contact. I'll tell reception that the next commitment is tomorrow's update, not completed work.
Lab coordinator: Thank you. I'll address that update to the lead assistant and identify anything still awaiting the dentist's response.
Vocabulary in this exchange: laboratory prescription: The authorized clinician's instructions to a dental laboratory for a specific case. / shade: The specified color designation used in matching a dental restoration.
END REFERENCE

Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.

Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.

Dialogue 08: A callback arrangement that can actually work
Original fictional setting: A patient discusses a missed callback with an assistant; clinical questions remain with the dentist.
Professional roles: Patient / Assistant
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Patient: I missed the dentist's call because I was on a train. Can someone call again about my postoperative instructions?
Assistant: I can pass on the callback request. Is it the same question about the phrase you identified earlier?
Patient: Yes, the same wording question. I haven't received an explanation yet, and I don't want it marked as answered.
Assistant: I'll record that the call was missed and the question remains unresolved, not that advice was received.
Patient: The number in my profile is correct, but I won't be free until after three. Can you promise a call then?
Assistant: I can record your availability, but I need the clinician to confirm a callback window before promising a time.
Patient: Please don't call my work reception instead. I only want the usual private contact arrangement used.
Assistant: I'll preserve that preference and flag it with your availability. A new contact method won't be assumed.
Patient: Does requesting another callback cancel the review appointment already booked for next week, or does that remain separate?
Assistant: It remains separate unless the appropriate team confirms a change. I'll ask reception to verify the existing booking too.
Patient: Thank you. Please tell me when a time is actually agreed, rather than saying the call is already booked.
Assistant: I will. If you report a new urgent concern, we'll follow our designated urgent route rather than this routine callback request.
Vocabulary in this exchange: postoperative instructions: The authorized guidance given after a dental procedure, which staff must not improvise or change. / review appointment: A planned follow-up visit for the clinician to assess or discuss care. / callback: A return contact arranged to respond to a patient's question or request. / urgent concern: A time-sensitive issue that must follow the practice's designated urgent or emergency route.
END REFERENCE

REFERENCE
Course: Dental Assisting English
Professional audience: Dental assistants and dental support staff
Scope: All patients, staff, records, schedules, and cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not dental treatment, radiographic interpretation, infection-control certification, or a universal scope of practice. Follow current local law, authorized duties, training, manufacturer instructions, and practice procedures. Use the appropriate urgent or emergency route for urgent concerns; routine callback examples do not replace it. United States and UK sources provide context, not interchangeable legal rules.
Lesson 01: Clarifying the appointment purpose
Original fictional case: Patient Lena arrives expecting a filling today. Assistant Amir sees a thirty-minute consultation on the schedule; no procedure is confirmed. The dentist is available to clarify the purpose before Lena decides whether to continue. Amir should acknowledge the time she has set aside, explain what the record actually shows, and preserve her questions about options, costs, and timing. He cannot promise a filling today, diagnose the tooth, or imply that attending a consultation means agreeing to whatever treatment might later be recommended.
Language workshop: A consultation is not a confirmed filling
Communication goal: Reconcile a patient expectation with the schedule and arrange an explanation before assuming a treatment decision.
Language guidance: A thirty-minute booking describes allocated time, not clinical suitability or consent for a filling. Explain the visit type and arrange the clinical discussion. Do not convert a hoped-for treatment into a confirmed procedure because the patient has arrived.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand you expected a filling today. / The schedule shows a thirty-minute consultation. / That differs from the treatment you were expecting. / Let us clarify the appointment before making assumptions. / What would you like the dentist to address first? / The dentist can discuss the purpose and options with you. / No filling is confirmed in the booking I can see. / You can ask questions before deciding whether to continue. / I cannot determine the treatment from the schedule. / Coming to this visit is not agreement to every possible procedure. / I will include your question about the treatment estimate. / Any coverage estimate needs confirmation through the appropriate process. / The allocated thirty minutes does not guarantee a procedure today. / Are we clear that the current booking is for consultation? / I will tell the dentist that you expected treatment today. / We will clarify the plan and your questions before treating a procedure as agreed.
Vocabulary: consultation: An appointment for discussion and assessment, not automatic agreement to treatment. / restoration: Repair or replacement of damaged tooth structure using an appropriate dental method. / filling: Material placed to restore a prepared tooth area as clinically indicated. / appointment type: The category of visit recorded in the schedule. / chief concern: The main issue the patient wants addressed. / presenting complaint: The problem or symptom reported at the visit. / clinical examination: Assessment performed by the appropriate clinician. / treatment plan: The proposed sequence of dental care discussed with the patient. / treatment option: A possible approach requiring appropriate explanation and assessment. / informed consent: Agreement through the applicable process after relevant understandable information. / provisional recommendation: A recommendation not yet finalized or confirmed. / definitive treatment: Treatment intended to address the identified condition rather than an interim measure. / caries: Tooth decay; identification and management require clinical assessment. / restorative dentistry: Dental care concerned with repairing or restoring teeth and function. / procedure code: A coded description of a dental service within a particular system. / treatment estimate: An estimated cost for specified proposed care. / coverage estimate: An estimate of relevant payment coverage, not a guarantee. / referral: A request for assessment or care by another appropriate provider. / review appointment: A follow-up visit to reassess an issue or treatment. / booking discrepancy: A difference between the recorded booking and the expected visit. / clinical decision: A decision requiring the appropriate clinical assessment and authority. / appointment duration: The allocated length of the visit. / patient expectation: What the patient believes will happen. / decision time: Time for the person to receive information and make the relevant choice.
Speaking task: Read Lena and Amir aloud; stress consultation and no procedure confirmed. Repeat the exchange with the crown-fitting facts below. Keep the twenty-minute duration separate from readiness. Check that neither speaker promises treatment or insurance payment; use the numbered answers to correct the four blanks.
Partner: Lena: Patient / Amir: Dental assistant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A patient expects a crown fitting. The schedule shows a twenty-minute review, and the dentist has not confirmed fitting readiness. The patient asks about timing and the estimate.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Lena: Then I would like to ask the dentist to explain what can be decided today and whether another appointment might be needed.
Amir: That is a useful question about the treatment plan. I will make sure it reaches the dentist rather than promise that today's allocated time can accommodate any treatment that may be recommended.
Lena: All right, I will speak to the dentist first. Please mention the time off work too. Another appointment would take some arranging.
Amir: I will. We will allow decision time for that discussion and keep the current status clear: consultation booked, procedure unconfirmed, and your questions still to be answered.
END REFERENCE

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.
